<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above" style="font-weight: normal; background-color: gold;"><span style="font-size:80%">Papal styles of</span><br /><b><span class="fn">Pope Urban V</span></b></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:C_o_a_Urbano_V.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/C_o_a_Urbano_V.svg/60px-C_o_a_Urbano_V.svg.png" decoding="async" width="60" height="73" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/C_o_a_Urbano_V.svg/90px-C_o_a_Urbano_V.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/39/C_o_a_Urbano_V.svg/120px-C_o_a_Urbano_V.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1143" data-file-height="1384" /></a></span></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Style_(manner_of_address)" class="mw-redirect" title="Style (manner of address)">Reference style</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/His_Holiness" class="mw-redirect" title="His Holiness">His Holiness</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Spoken style</th><td class="infobox-data">Your Holiness</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Religious style</th><td class="infobox-data">Holy Father</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Posthumous style</th><td class="infobox-data">None</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Pope Urban V</b> (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Urbanus V</i>; 1310 – 19 December 1370), born <b>Guillaume de Grimoard</b>,<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> was the head of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> from 28 September 1362 until his death, in December 1370 and was also a member of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Saint_Benedict" class="mw-redirect" title="Order of Saint Benedict">Order of Saint Benedict</a>. He was the only <a href="/wiki/Avignon_pope" class="mw-redirect" title="Avignon pope">Avignon pope</a> to be <a href="/wiki/Beatified" class="mw-redirect" title="Beatified">beatified</a>.
</p><p>Even after his election as pontiff, he continued to follow the Benedictine Rule, living simply and modestly. His habits did not always gain him supporters who were used to lives of affluence.
</p><p>Urban V pressed for reform throughout his pontificate and also oversaw the restoration and construction of churches and monasteries. One of the goals he set himself upon his election to the Papacy was the reunion of the Eastern and Western Churches.<sup id="cite_ref-americancatholic.org_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-americancatholic.org-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> He came as close as some of his predecessors and successors, but did not succeed.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Urban_V&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Guillaume de Grimoard was born in 1310 in the <a href="/wiki/Castle_of_Grizac" class="mw-redirect" title="Castle of Grizac">Castle of Grizac</a> in the <a href="/wiki/France" title="France">French</a> region of <a href="/wiki/Languedoc" title="Languedoc">Languedoc</a> (today part of the <a href="/wiki/Communes_of_France" title="Communes of France">commune</a> of <a href="/wiki/Le_Pont-de-Montvert" title="Le Pont-de-Montvert">Le Pont-de-Montvert</a>, <a href="/wiki/Departments_of_France" title="Departments of France">department</a> of <a href="/wiki/Loz%C3%A8re" title="Lozère">Lozère</a>), the second son of Guillaume de Grimoard, Lord of Bellegarde, and of Amphélise de Montferrand.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> He had two brothers, Étienne and <a href="/wiki/Angel_de_Grimoard" title="Angel de Grimoard">Angel</a> the future cardinal, and a sister Delphine.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1327, Guillaume Grimoard became a <a href="/wiki/Benedictine" class="mw-redirect" title="Benedictine">Benedictine</a> <a href="/wiki/Monk" title="Monk">monk</a> in the small <a href="/wiki/Chirac,_Loz%C3%A8re" title="Chirac, Lozère">Priory of Chirac</a>, near his home,<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> which was a <a href="/wiki/Dependency_(religion)" title="Dependency (religion)">dependency</a> of the ancient <a href="/wiki/Abbey_of_St._Victor,_Marseille" class="mw-redirect" title="Abbey of St. Victor, Marseille">Abbey of St. Victor</a> near <a href="/wiki/Marseille" title="Marseille">Marseille</a>. He was sent to St. Victor for his <a href="/wiki/Novitiate" title="Novitiate">novitiate</a>. After his profession of monastic vows, he was <a href="/wiki/Holy_Orders" class="mw-redirect" title="Holy Orders">ordained</a> a priest in his own monastery in Chirac in 1334. He studied literature and law at Montpellier, and then he moved to the University of Toulouse, where he studied law for four years. He earned a doctorate in <a href="/wiki/Canon_law_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Canon law (Catholic Church)">Canon Law</a> on 31 October 1342.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was appointed Prior of Nôtre-Dame du Pré (de Priorato) in the diocese of Auxerre by Pope Clement VI, which he held until his promotion to Saint-Germain en Auxerre in 1352.  He began both disciplinary and financial reforms. His new bishop, Jean d'Auxois (1353–1359), however, in concert with the Archbishop of Sens, Guillaume de Melun, made heavy demands on their hospitality, and when the latter attempted to impose new exactions, which were resisted by Grimoard, the Archbishop physically abused the Prior, who nonetheless would not submit.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>  Prior Grimoard became Procurator-General for the Order of St. Benedict at the Papal Curia.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He became a noted <a href="/wiki/Canonist" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonist">canonist</a>,  teaching at Montpellier, Paris and <a href="/wiki/Avignon" title="Avignon">Avignon</a>. He was appointed by the Bishop of Clermont, Pierre de Aigrefeuille (1349–1357), to be his vicar general, which meant in effect that he ruled the diocese on behalf of the bishop. When Bishop Pierre was transferred to Uzès (1357–1366), Guillaume Grimond became Vicar General of Uzès.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Guillaume was named abbot of the monastery of Saint-Germain en Auxerre on 13 February 1352 by Pope Clement VI.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>  In 1359 the town and abbey were captured by the English and subjected to heavy imposts.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Italian_missions">Italian missions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Urban_V&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Italian missions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="First_mission">First mission</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Urban_V&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: First mission">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In the summer of 1352  <a href="/wiki/Pope_Clement_VI" title="Pope Clement VI">Pope Clement VI</a> summoned Abbot Guillaume for an assignment.  Northern Italy had been in a chaotic state for some time, due to the ambitions of the Visconti of Milan, led by Archbishop Giovanni Visconti.  He had conquered much of Lombardy, seized the Papal city of Bologna, and was invading the borders of Florentine territory. In order to keep a hold on the territory for the Catholic Church, the Pope had hit on the scheme of making Archbishop Visconti his vicar of Bologna for the present. He drew up an agreement on 27 April 1352, which absolved the Visconti of all their transgressions and signed away much of northern Italy.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The Pope even made the first payment on the subsidy which he was going to provide them. The Visconti, on their part, had no intention of observing the terms of the pact, one of which was the return of the Legation of Bologna to the Papacy, despite the fine words and promises they made in Avignon. On 26 July, Abbot Grimoard and Msgr. Azzo Manzi da Reggio, the Dean of the Cathedral of Aquileia, were presented with written instructions by Pope Clement to go to northern Italy as apostolic nuncios to deal with the situation. Guillaume was to receive the city of Bologna from the Visconti, who were illegal occupiers, and hand it over to Giovanni Visconti as the papal vicar, and to threaten with ecclesiastical censures any parties who did not adhere to the treaty.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>  This he did on 2 October 1352. Guillaume was allotted 8 gold florins a day for his expenses, his associate Anzo only 4 florins. While he was in Milan he was also able to get the Archbishop to renew the treaty that was expiring with the King and Queen of Sicily.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Second_mission">Second mission</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Urban_V&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Second mission">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1354 Abbot Grimoard was sent to Italy again, this time to Rome, where there was business that needed to be transacted for the Apostolic Camera. There were also serious disorders in the Basilica of St. Peter which needed to be sorted out.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In August 1361, he was elected the <a href="/wiki/Abbot" title="Abbot">abbot</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Abbey_of_St._Victor,_Marseille" class="mw-redirect" title="Abbey of St. Victor, Marseille">Abbey of Saint-Victor</a> in Marseille.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Despite the appointment, he continued to teach as a professor, at least for the next academic year.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Third_mission">Third mission</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Urban_V&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Third mission">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Gil_%C3%81lvarez_Carrillo_de_Albornoz" title="Gil Álvarez Carrillo de Albornoz">Gil Álvarez Carrillo de Albornoz</a> had been sent to Italy in 1353, to bring under control the notorious <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_di_Vico" title="Giovanni di Vico">Giovanni di Vico</a> of Viterbo, as well as the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Malatesta" title="House of Malatesta">Malatesta</a> of Rimini and the <a href="/wiki/Ordelaffi" class="mw-redirect" title="Ordelaffi">Ordelaffi</a> family of <a href="/wiki/Forl%C3%AC" title="Forlì">Forlì</a>. In 1360 Abbot Guillaume was sent to assist him by dealing with Archbishop Visconti's nephew and successor, <a href="/wiki/Bernab%C3%B2_Visconti" title="Bernabò Visconti">Bernabò Visconti</a>. Their confrontation was so hostile and threatening that the Abbot left immediately and reported back to Pope Innocent the treachery of his vassal. The Pope sent him back to Italy immediately, but happily the utter defeat of Visconti's army which was besieging Bologna by Cardinal Albornoz eased the situation considerably.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Nonetheless, immediately after he was elected pope, Grimoard excommunicated Bernabò Visconti.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> He returned to France, and retired to his castle of Auriol, where he was found on 10 June 1362.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The reason for his retirement to Auriol is not far to seek.  The plague was raging in southern France again in 1361 and 1362.   Cardinal Pierre des Près died on 16 May 1361; Cardinal Petrus de Foresta, died on 7 June 1361; Cardinal Guillaume Farinier, died on 17 June 1361; Cardinal Guillaume Court, O.Cist., died on  12 June 1361; Cardinal Petrus Bertrandi, died on 13 July 1361; Cardinal Jean de Caraman, died on 1 August 1361; Cardinal Bernard de la Tour, died on 7 August 1361; Cardinal Francesco degli Atti, died on 25 August 1361; and Cardinal Pierre de Cros died in September 1361.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>  In addition it was estimated that some 6000 persons and more than 100 bishops died in 1361.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>  Cardinal Nicolas Roselli (1357–1362) of Tarragona died at Majorca on 28 March 1362, though not of the plague.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Naples">Naples</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Urban_V&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Naples">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>King <a href="/wiki/Louis_I_of_Naples" title="Louis I of Naples">Louis I of Naples</a> died on 25 May 1362.  This set off a power struggle, with <a href="/wiki/Joanna_I_of_Naples" title="Joanna I of Naples">Queen Joanna I</a> attempting to get back the power she had lost to her husband, as well as a contest to see who her next husband would be.  Abbot Guillaume was summoned to Avignon, where he was on 27 June, and sent to Naples to provide the advice and guidance as to the desires of the feudal overlord of Naples, Pope Innocent VI.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his trip to the south, he visited the great Benedictine abbey of <a href="/wiki/Monte_Cassino" title="Monte Cassino">Monte Cassino</a>, where he was saddened to see the state into which it had fallen, both physically and organizationally, both from earthquakes and episcopal neglect.  As soon as he became Pope he undertook to repair the situation,<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> and on 31 March 1367 he abolished the diocese of Cassino and restored the monastery to the complete control of its Abbot.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Papacy">Papacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Urban_V&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Papacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Papal_conclave,_1362" class="mw-redirect" title="Papal conclave, 1362">Papal conclave, 1362</a></div>
<p>In September 1362, Grimoard was apostolic nuncio in Italy when <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_VI" title="Pope Innocent VI">Pope Innocent VI</a> died. Exactly where he was when the news reached him summoning him to Avignon is unknown.  Naples is just a guess; other possibilities are Florence and Lombardy.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pope Innocent VI died on 12 September 1362.  The Conclave to elect his successor opened on 22 September, the Feast of <a href="/wiki/Saint_Maurice" title="Saint Maurice">Saint Maurice</a>, in the Apostolic Palace in Avignon.  Twenty of the twenty-one cardinals were in attendance.  Only Cardinal Albornoz remained at his post in Italy.  Of the twenty cardinals eighteen were French in origin, six of them Limousin. Ten of the twenty-one cardinals were papal relatives. The influence of the Limousin cardinals was somewhat diminished since their homeland had recently become subject to English occupation, which frightened the thirteen cardinals who were subjects of the King of France.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>  Both Cardinals Hélie de Talleyrand and Guy de Boulogne considered themselves to be electable.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Matteo Villani, the Florentine chronicler, says that fifteen cardinals were prepared to elect, or actually elected, Hugues Roger, OSB, a Limousin and the brother of Pope Clement VI, who was Chamberlain of the College of Cardinals.  Cardinal Hugues declined the offer.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>  Villani is the only source that reports this version of events. This story, moreover, contradicts the report of Jean de Froissart,<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> who claims that a stalemate developed between Talleyrand and Guy de Boulogne, such that members of neither party could get the required two-thirds of the votes. It was apparently one of the Limousin Cardinals, Guillaume d'Aigrefeuille, who directed the attention of the cardinals to Abbot Guillaume Grimoard.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> On 28 September, they elected Grimoard as the new Pope.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> He was not initially informed of the result; instead, he was requested to return immediately to Avignon to "consult" with the Conclave.  The cardinals feared the reaction of the Romans to the election of another French pope, and so kept the results of the election secret until Grimoard's arrival a month later, at the end of October. The Romans had been clamoring for some time for a Roman, or at least Italian, pope, and it was feared they would interfere with Guillaume's travel had they known of his election.<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Upon his arrival, Grimoard accepted his election and took the pontifical name of Urban V.  When asked the reason for the selection of his new name, Grimoard was alleged to have said: "All the popes who have borne this name were saints".<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Grimoard was not even a bishop at the time of his election, and had to be <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_consecration" class="mw-redirect" title="Episcopal consecration">consecrated</a> before he could be crowned. This was done on 6 November by Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Andouin_Aubert" title="Andouin Aubert">Andouin Aubert</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> the Bishop of Ostia,  a nephew of Grimoard's predecessor, Innocent VI. The Bishop of Ostia had the traditional right to consecrate a pope a bishop.  At the conclusion of the consecration Mass, Urban V was crowned. There is no record of who it was who placed the crown on his head.  The right to do so belonged to the cardinal protodeacon, who was Cardinal Guillaume de la Jugié, a nephew of Pope Clement VI.  Urban V was the sixth pope in the <a href="/wiki/Avignon_Papacy" title="Avignon Papacy">Avignon Papacy</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Urban V kept on another papal nephew, Arnaud Aubert, the nephew of Pope Innocent VI.  He had been given the very important position of papal chamberlain, the head of the church's financial department, by his uncle in 1361.  He continued in that office throughout the reign of Urban V and also that of Gregory XI, until 1371.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> In addition to the management of the papal household, the office made Aubert the temporal vicar for the Pope in the diocese of Avignon and the administrator of the Comtat-Venaissin.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1363–1364 the winter was so cold, especially in January, February and March, that the Rhone froze over to the extent that people and vehicles could travel across the ice.  The Pope, however, announced that he would excommunicate anyone who attempted to do so, fearing that people might accidentally fall in and be drowned.  Near Carcassonne, a man froze to death while travelling on his horse, though the horse was able to make it back to its accustomed stable with the dead man on its back.  Many of the poor, women, and children died of the cold.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:BologninoUrbanoV.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/BologninoUrbanoV.jpg/240px-BologninoUrbanoV.jpg" decoding="async" width="240" height="119" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/BologninoUrbanoV.jpg/360px-BologninoUrbanoV.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/BologninoUrbanoV.jpg/480px-BologninoUrbanoV.jpg 2x" data-file-width="500" data-file-height="247" /></a><figcaption>A <i><a href="/wiki/Bolognino" title="Bolognino">bolognino</a></i> of Urban V</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Reformer_and_patron_of_education">Reformer and patron of education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Urban_V&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Reformer and patron of education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>As pope, Urban V continued to follow the discipline of the <a href="/wiki/Benedictine_Rule" class="mw-redirect" title="Benedictine Rule">Benedictine Rule</a> and to wear his <a href="/wiki/Monastic_habit" class="mw-redirect" title="Monastic habit">monastic habit</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Urban V worked against absenteeism, pluralism and simony,  while seeking to improve clerical training and examination.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>  It must be kept in mind, however, that, with the training of a monk, reform was a matter of return to ideal values and principles through discipline, not a matter of striking out with new solutions.  With the training of a lawyer, reform was a matter of codifying and enforcing established decisions and precedents.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Pope Urban V introduced considerable reforms in the administration of justice and liberally patronized learning. He founded a university in <a href="/wiki/Hungary" title="Hungary">Hungary</a>. He granted the University of Pavia the status of Studium Generale (14 April 1363).<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> In <a href="/wiki/University_of_Toulouse" title="University of Toulouse">Toulouse</a>, he granted the Theology Faculty the same rights as possessed by the University of Paris.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> In <a href="/wiki/Montpellier" title="Montpellier">Montpellier</a>, he restored the school of <a href="/wiki/Medicine" title="Medicine">medicine</a>  and founded the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Montpellier" title="University of Montpellier">College of Saint Benedict</a>, whose church, decorated with numerous works of art, later became the cathedral of the city. He founded a collegiate church in <a href="/wiki/Qu%C3%A9zac,_Loz%C3%A8re" title="Quézac, Lozère">Quézac</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> and a church and library in <a href="/wiki/Ispagnac" title="Ispagnac">Ispagnac</a>.  On a hilltop near <a href="/wiki/B%C3%A9dou%C3%A8s" title="Bédouès">Bédouès</a>, the parish in which the Château de Grisac is situated, he built a church where the bodies of his parents were buried, and, we are informed by a papal bull of December 1363, he instituted a college of six canon-priests, along with a deacon and a subdeacon.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Urban V issued a preliminary consent for the establishment of the University of <a href="/wiki/Krak%C3%B3w" title="Kraków">Kraków</a>, which by September 1364 had gained full papal consent.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> He provided books and the best professors to more than 1,000 students of all classes. Around <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>, he also planted <a href="/wiki/Vineyards" class="mw-redirect" title="Vineyards">vineyards</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>He imposed the penalty of excommunication on anyone who molested the Jews or attempted forcible conversion and baptism.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Military_campaigns">Military campaigns</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Urban_V&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Military campaigns">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The great feature of Urban V's reign was the effort to return the <a href="/wiki/Papacy" class="mw-redirect" title="Papacy">papacy</a> to Rome and to suppress its powerful rivals for the temporal sovereignty there. He began by sending his brother, Cardinal Angelicus Grimoard, as <a href="/wiki/Papal_legate" title="Papal legate">legate</a> in northern Italy.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>  In 1362 Urban ordered a crusade to be preached throughout <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a> against Bernabò Visconti, Giangaleazzo Visconti and their kindred, accused as robbers of the church's estate. In March 1363 Bernabò was declared a heretic.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> However, Pope Urban found it necessary to purchase peace in March of the following year, sending the newly created Cardinal Androin de la Roche, former Abbot of Cluny, as apostolic legate to Italy to arrange the business.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> Then, through the mediation of <a href="/wiki/Emperor_Charles_IV" class="mw-redirect" title="Emperor Charles IV">Emperor Charles IV</a>, Urban lifted his excommunication against Bernabò, obtaining Bologna only after he signed a hasty peace that was highly favorable to Bernabò.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2020)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In May 1365 the Emperor Charles visited Avignon, where he appeared with the Pope in full imperial regalia.  He then proceeded to Arles, which was one of his domains, where he was crowned King by the Archbishop, <a href="/wiki/Pierre_de_Murat_de_Cros" title="Pierre de Murat de Cros">Pierre de Cros</a>, OSB.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Urban V's greatest desire was that of a crusade against the Turks. In 1363, <a href="/wiki/John_II_of_France" title="John II of France">King John II</a> of France and <a href="/wiki/Peter_I_of_Cyprus#Tour_of_Europe" title="Peter I of Cyprus">Peter I, the King of Cyprus</a>, came to Avignon, and it was decided that there should be a war against the Turks.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> It was Urban and Peter who were most eager for the crusade; the French were exhausted by recent losses in the Hundred Years' War, and some of their leaders were still being held prisoner in England. The Pope held a special ceremony on Holy Saturday, 1363, and bestowed the crusader's cross on the two kings, and on Cardinal <a href="/wiki/H%C3%A9lie_de_Talleyrand-P%C3%A9rigord_(cardinal)" title="Hélie de Talleyrand-Périgord (cardinal)">Hélie de Talleyrand</a> as well.  John II was appointed Rector and Captain General of the expedition.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> Cardinal de Talleyrand was appointed apostolic legate for the expedition, but he died on 17 January 1364,  before the expedition could set out.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Assembling the army proved an impossible task, and King John returned to prison in England.  He died in London on 8 April 1364.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Urbain_V_by_JM_Rosier.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Urbain_V_by_JM_Rosier.jpg/200px-Urbain_V_by_JM_Rosier.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="466" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Urbain_V_by_JM_Rosier.jpg/300px-Urbain_V_by_JM_Rosier.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/Urbain_V_by_JM_Rosier.jpg/400px-Urbain_V_by_JM_Rosier.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1764" data-file-height="4110" /></a><figcaption>Urban V, <a href="/wiki/Abbey_of_St._Victor,_Marseille" class="mw-redirect" title="Abbey of St. Victor, Marseille">Abbey of St. Victor, Marseille</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>King Peter of Cyprus, disappointed by King John's return to captivity in England and the death of Cardinal de Talleyrand, collected whatever soldiers he could, and  in 1365 launched a successful attack on <a href="/wiki/Alexandria" title="Alexandria">Alexandria</a> (11 October 1365).  Additional support was not forthcoming, however, and seeing that the enemy vastly outnumbered the crusaders, he ordered the sacking and burning of the city, and then withdrew.  He continued to harass the coasts of Syria and Egypt until he was assassinated in 1369. Urban, however, played no part in the crusade or its aftermath.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Amadeus of Savoy and Louis of Hungary also put together a crusade in Urban's reign in 1366.  Initially they were successful, and Amadeus even captured <a href="/wiki/Gallipoli" title="Gallipoli">Gallipoli</a>.  But despite initial successes, each was forced to withdraw.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="To_Rome_and_back">To Rome and back</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Urban_V&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: To Rome and back">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Continued troubles in Italy, as well as pleas from figures such as <a href="/wiki/Petrarch" title="Petrarch">Petrarch</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bridget_of_Sweden" title="Bridget of Sweden">Bridget of Sweden</a>, caused Urban V to set out for <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a>, only to find that his Vicar, Cardinal Albornoz, had just died.  He conducted the remains of the Cardinal to Assisi, where they were buried in the Basilica of Saint Francis.  The Pope reached the City of Rome on 16 October 1367, the first pope in sixty years to set foot in his own diocese.  He was greeted by the clergy and people with joy, and despite the satisfaction of being attended by the <a href="/wiki/Charles_IV,_Holy_Roman_Emperor" title="Charles IV, Holy Roman Emperor">Emperor Charles IV</a> in <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Basilica" title="St. Peter&#39;s Basilica">St. Peter's</a>, and of placing the crown upon the head of the <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_of_Pomerania" title="Elizabeth of Pomerania">Empress Elizabeth</a> (1 November 1368),<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> it soon became clear that by changing the seat of his government he had not increased its power. In Rome he was nonetheless able to receive the homage of King <a href="/wiki/Peter_I_of_Cyprus" title="Peter I of Cyprus">Peter I of Cyprus</a>, Queen <a href="/wiki/Joan_I_of_Naples" class="mw-redirect" title="Joan I of Naples">Joan I of Naples</a>, and the confession of faith by the Byzantine Emperor <a href="/wiki/John_V_Palaeologus" class="mw-redirect" title="John V Palaeologus">John V Palaeologus</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>  Bridget of Sweden, who was living in Rome and attempting to get approval for a new religious order,  the <a href="/wiki/Bridgettines" title="Bridgettines">Bridgettines</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> had actually appeared before the Pope at Montefiascone in 1370 as he was preparing to return to France, and, in the presence of Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_XI" title="Pope Gregory XI">Pierre Roger de Beaufort</a>, the future pope, predicted the death of the Pope if he should leave Rome.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Unable any longer to resist the urgency of the French cardinals, and despite several cities of the Papal States still being in revolt, Urban V boarded a ship at <a href="/wiki/Corneto" class="mw-redirect" title="Corneto">Corneto</a> heading for France on 5 September 1370, arriving back at <a href="/wiki/Avignon" title="Avignon">Avignon</a> on the 27th of the same month.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERollo-Koster2008181-182_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERollo-Koster2008181-182-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> A few days later he fell severely ill. Feeling his death approaching, he asked that he might be moved from the Papal Palace to the nearby residence of his brother, <a href="/wiki/Angel_de_Grimoard" title="Angel de Grimoard">Angelic de Grimoard</a>, whom he had made a cardinal, that he might be close to those he loved.<sup id="cite_ref-americancatholic.org_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-americancatholic.org-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> He died there on 19 December 1370.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Osborne1991_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Osborne1991-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> He had been pope for eight years, one month, and nineteen days.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>  His body was initially placed in the Chapel of John XXII in the Cathedral of S. Marie de Domps in Avignon. On 31 May 1371 his remains were transferred to  the monastery of Saint-Victor in Marseille, where he had built a splendid tomb for himself.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Beatification">Beatification</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Urban_V&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Beatification">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" style="background-color:gold;"><div class="fn">Urban V</div><br /><div class="honorific-prefix" style="display:inline;font-size: 77%; font-weight: normal;"><a href="/wiki/Order_of_Saint_Benedict" class="mw-redirect" title="Order of Saint Benedict">O.S.B.</a></div></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><span class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a href="/wiki/File:Papa_Urbanus_Quintus.jpg" class="infobox-caption">Portrait - Henri Auguste Calixte César Serrur.</div></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color:gold;">Pope; Confessor</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Born</th><td class="infobox-data">Guillaume de Grimoard<br />1310<br /><a href="/wiki/Ch%C3%A2teau_de_Grizac" title="Château de Grizac">Château de Grizac</a>, <a href="/wiki/Le_Pont-de-Montvert" title="Le Pont-de-Montvert">Le Pont-de-Montvert</a>, <a href="/wiki/Languedoc" title="Languedoc">Languedoc</a>, <a href="/wiki/France_in_the_Middle_Ages" title="France in the Middle Ages">Kingdom of France</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">19 December 1370 (aged 60)<br /><a href="/wiki/Avignon" title="Avignon">Avignon</a>, <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Venerated&#160;in</th><td class="infobox-data"><a of saints">Feast</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/19_December" class="mw-redirect" title="19 December">19 December</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Saint_symbolism" title="Saint symbolism">Attributes</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Papal_regalia_and_insignia" title="Papal regalia and insignia">Papal vestments</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Papal_tiara" title="Papal tiara">Papal tiara</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">Patronage</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Architect" title="Architect">Architects</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Teacher" title="Teacher">Educators</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Benedictines" title="Benedictines">Benedictines</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Missionary" title="Missionary">Missionaries</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><a href="/wiki/Pope_Gregory_XI" title="Pope Gregory XI">Pope Gregory XI</a> opened the cause of beatification of his predecessor. Urban V's claimed miracles and his virtues were documented.<sup id="cite_ref-Osborne1991_59-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Osborne1991-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> But the cause stopped in 1379 in Rome. It stopped in Avignon in 1390, under the orders of the antipope <a href="/wiki/Antipope_Clement_VII" title="Antipope Clement VII">Clement VII</a>. The Western Schism caused the process to stop, but it was revived centuries later, and led to the beatification of Urban V on 10 March 1870 by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_IX" title="Pope Pius IX">Pope Pius IX</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_5-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> His feast day is celebrated on 19 December, the day of his death. This was decided upon by a General Chapter of the Benedictine Order held in 1414.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="See_also">See also</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Urban_V&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: See also">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="References">References</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Urban_V&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: References">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

Olive. p.&#160;446.</cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Histoire+de+Marseille&amp;rft.place=Marseille&amp;rft.pages=446&amp;rft.pub=M.+Olive&amp;rft.date=1829&amp;rft.au=Augustin+Fabre&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DhIc5_QNv9cQC%26pg%3DPA446&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3APope+Urban+V" class="Z3988"></span> The 'Third Life' of Urban V, by Petrus de Herentals, says: apud Lombardiam existente in legationem ('while serving on his Legation in Lombardy'). <li id="cite_note-55"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-55">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">She had founded a community in Sweden in 1346, but had refused to follow the regulations of the IV Lateran Council, that new orders had to adopt the Rule of some already established Order. Finally, against her wishes, the nuns adopted the Rule of S. Augustine, though <a href="/wiki/Pope_Urban_VI" title="Pope Urban VI">Pope Urban VI</a> in 1378 allowed her rule to be incorporated in the Rule of S. Augustine. <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFPhilip_Sheldrake2005" class="citation book cs1">Philip Sheldrake (2005). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=bPOft7krR84C&amp;pg=PA157"><i>The New Westminster Dictionary of Christian Spirituality</i></a>. <li id="cite_note-Osborne1991-59"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-Osborne1991_59-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-Osborne1991_59-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFOsborne1991" class="citation journal cs1">Osborne, John (1991). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.jstor.org/stable/1482514">"Lost Roman Images of Pope Urban V (1362-1370) for Julian Gardner"</a>. <i>Zeitschrift für Kunstgeschichte</i>. <b>54</b> (1): 20–32. <a href="/wiki/Doi_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="Doi (identifier)">doi</a>:<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://doi.org/10.2307%2F1482514">10.2307/1482514</a>. <a href="/wiki/JSTOR_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="JSTOR (identifier)">JSTOR</a>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.jstor.org/stable/1482514">1482514</a><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">18 December</span> 2020</span>. <q>Contemporary sources indicate that, within three days of Urban's death on 19 December 1370, word had begun to spread of miracles secured by his intervention, and those who had obtained his assistance began to bring wax votive images and candles which they deposited at his tomb. In 1372, Urban's remains were transferred from Avignonto the monastery of St. Victor at Marseilles, where he had formerly served as abbot. By this time his cult had become well known in southern France, and large crowds lined the route. Miracles accredited to Urban were soon being recorded in many parts of western Europe, from Flanders and Bohemia in the north, to Spain and Italy in the south, although the majority seem to have taken place in the vicinity of Marseilles, where the relics now reposed.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=article&amp;rft.jtitle=Zeitschrift+f%C3%BCr+Kunstgeschichte&amp;rft.atitle=Lost+Roman+Images+of+Pope+Urban+V+%281362-1370%29+for+Julian+Gardner&amp;rft.volume=54&amp;rft.issue=1&amp;rft.pages=20-32&amp;rft.date=1991&amp;rft_id=info%3Adoi%2F10.2307%2F1482514&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.jstor.org%2Fstable%2F1482514%23id-name%3DJSTOR&amp;rft.aulast=Osborne&amp;rft.aufirst=John&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.jstor.org%2Fstable%2F1482514&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3APope+Urban+V" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-60"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-60">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Baluze (1693), I, p. 363.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-61"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-61">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Baluze (1693), I, pp. 413 and 417.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-62"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-62">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Albanès, pp. 124–374. Much of the material can be ascribed to spiritual enthusiasm and an absence of verification.</span>
